Funding for the Department of Veterans Affairs medical care accounts is limited only by the nondefense limit as increased by the rate of inflation.

(e) LIMIT ON THE VALUE OF TAX PREFERENCES.—Section 68 of the Internal Revenue Code of 1986 is amended so that, for taxable years beginning after December 31, 2027, for a taxpayer whose adjusted gross income exceeds $400,000, the tax benefit of itemized deductions and of exclusions for employer-provided health coverage, retirement contributions, and tax-exempt interest is limited to twenty-eight percent (28%) of the amount deducted or excluded.

(f) CARBON FEE SCHEDULE.—Section 2410(b) is amended so that the rate increases by five dollars ($5) per metric ton each year, beginning in the second year, until it reaches seventy-five dollars ($75), and is indexed to the Consumer Price Index thereafter. The household energy credits of Section 2410(c)(1) remain at thirty percent (30%) of net receipts at every rate.

(g) PAYMENT INTEGRITY.—Each agency with a program reporting improper payments above one billion dollars ($1,000,000,000) under section 3352 of title 31, United States Code, shall reduce its improper payment rate by not less than one-third within three (3) fiscal years, using the verification systems of Sections 3509 and 3904. Recoveries and avoided payments are applied to deficit reduction.

(h) CROP INSURANCE.—Section 508(e) of the Federal Crop Insurance Act (7 U.S.C. 1508(e)) is amended so that, beginning with the 2029 reinsurance year, the federal share of crop insurance premiums averages forty percent (40%), with the full current-law share retained for beginning, veteran, and socially disadvantaged farmers and for producers with adjusted gross income below two hundred fifty thousand dollars ($250,000); reimbursement of insurers' administrative and operating expenses is limited to nine and one-quarter percent (9.25%) of premiums; and the targeted rate of return to approved insurance providers is twelve percent (12%). Catastrophic coverage and disaster assistance are not affected.

(i) PROJECTED EFFECT.—The measures in subsections (b) through (h) are projected to reduce the deficit by approximately three hundred five billion dollars ($305,000,000,000) a year at full effect, stated in first-year dollars. The Social Security changes of subsection (b) phase in over a generation: they have little effect within the ten-year budget window and improve the long-range actuarial balance of the Social Security Trust Funds by approximately four-tenths of one percent (0.4%) of taxable payroll, as estimated from published provisions of the Office of the Chief Actuary. Together with Section 3406, ten-year deficit reduction is projected at approximately four trillion four hundred billion dollars ($4,400,000,000,000), or approximately five trillion dollars ($5,000,000,000,000) including reduced interest costs, as itemized in Schedule G. Under these projections the deficit falls from approximately six percent (6%) to approximately five percent (5%) of gross domestic product; the remaining reduction needed to meet the limits of Section 3406(f)(2) is to be provided by a Fiscal Target Act or by Section 3406(f)(4).

Sec. 3411. American Resource Fund

(a) ESTABLISHMENT.—There is established in the Treasury a permanent fund to be known as the American Resource Fund.

(b) DEPOSITS.—There shall be deposited in the Fund all federal receipts from royalties, rents, and bonus bids for minerals, oil, gas, and offshore wind on federal lands and the Outer Continental Shelf in excess of the amounts projected for those receipts in the baseline of the Congressional Budget Office for the fiscal year of enactment, including all additional receipts under Section 2414, together with the earnings of the Fund. Amounts required by law to be paid to States, Indian tribes, or the Land and Water Conservation Fund are not affected.

(c) INVESTMENT.—Until debt held by the public falls below ninety percent (90%) of gross domestic product, the Fund shall be invested only in interest-bearing obligations of the United States, so that its deposits reduce federal borrowing. Thereafter, a board of five (5) trustees appointed by the President with the advice and consent of the Senate, serving staggered seven-year terms and removable only for cause, may invest up to sixty percent (60%) of the Fund in a diversified portfolio of domestic equities and bonds through passive index investments, with no authority to vote shares or select individual companies.

(d) PRINCIPAL PROTECTED.—The principal of the Fund shall not be appropriated. Beginning in the eleventh fiscal year after enactment, one-half of the Fund's realized earnings each year shall be transferred to the Federal Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, and the remainder shall be retained in the Fund.

(e) BUDGETARY TREATMENT.—Investments of the Fund in non-federal assets shall be recorded on a fair-value basis in the manner of section 123 of the Emergency Economic Stabilization Act of 2008 (12 U.S.C. 5232).

(f) REPORTING.—The trustees shall publish an annual report on holdings, returns, and transfers, audited by the Comptroller General.

Sec. 3412. Expenditure Review and Savings Verification

(a) PURPOSE.—To make the federal government measurably more efficient at what it already pays for, and to ensure that no savings are claimed before they are real.

(b) EXPENDITURE REVIEWS.—The Comptroller General shall review each federal program or activity with outlays above one billion dollars ($1,000,000,000) a year at least once every five (5) years, beginning with programs identified in the Comptroller General's annual reports on fragmentation, overlap, and duplication. Each agency shall maintain a public inventory of its contracts, grants, facilities, information technology systems, and administrative costs.

(c) REMEDIATION PLANS.—For each open recommendation of the Comptroller General or an Inspector General with estimated financial benefits above one hundred million dollars ($100,000,000), the agency shall publish within one hundred eighty (180) days a remediation plan with a deadline and an estimate of savings, or a statement of reasons for not acting.

(d) SAVINGS RECOGNITION.—For purposes of this Act—

(1) savings are recognized only when the Congressional Budget Office has scored them or when actual reductions in outlays have been measured against an adjusted baseline and audited by the Comptroller General;

(2) no savings may be counted under more than one section of this Act;

(3) savings from the Strategic Defense Efficiency Commission under Section 2304, from upstream prevention under Section 3406(c), and from returns on federal investments are excluded from every projection of this Act until recognized under paragraph (1); and

(4) recognized net savings are applied first to deficit reduction until the limits of Section 3406(f) are met.

(e) INFRASTRUCTURE FINANCING.—Each project funded under Section 3406(c) shall be classified as—

(1) revenue-generating, including transmission, water systems with rate revenue, semiconductor and critical-materials facilities, and public compute, which shall be financed through loans, loan guarantees, or revenue-sharing agreements wherever practicable, with repayments returned to the Treasury and applied to deficit reduction;

(2) cost-saving, including flood mitigation, water remediation, and soil restoration, which shall state the future federal costs it is expected to avoid and report the results; or

(3) public-good, including wildlife corridors and essential rural services, which shall be funded through capped appropriations with a published statement of public benefits.

(f) PROTECTIONS.—Nothing in this section authorizes a reduction in benefits under Social Security, Medicare, Medicaid, veterans' programs, the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program, or the Basic Minimum Plan, or a reduction in military readiness.

(g) REPORT.—The Comptroller General shall report annually to Congress on reviews completed, remediation plans published, and savings recognized under this section.

Title XXXV · Public Integrity and Executive Accountability

Sec. 3501. Findings and Purpose

(a) FINDINGS.—Congress finds that:

(1) The Emoluments Clauses of the Constitution — Article I, Section 9, Clause 8 (Foreign Emoluments) and Article II, Section 1, Clause 7 (Domestic Emoluments) — prohibit the President and other federal officers from receiving personal financial benefits from foreign governments or from sources other than their official compensation, yet no adequate statutory enforcement mechanism with private right of action has existed to compel compliance;

(2) The practice of senior executive branch officials and their immediate family members maintaining active foreign business partnerships, licensing agreements, and revenue-sharing arrangements with foreign government-linked entities during their tenure in office creates structural conflicts of interest that compromise governmental decision-making on matters of trade, defense, and diplomatic policy;

(3) The sale or licensing of the presidential or vice-presidential identity, name, or brand for commercial purposes during a term of office to foreign nationals or foreign government-linked enterprises constitutes a per se violation of the Foreign Emoluments Clause regardless of the transactional form used;

(4) Current financial disclosure requirements under the Ethics in Government Act permit broad exemptions, delayed reporting, and valuation ranges that obscure the full scope of financial conflicts and do not reach family members' business dealings that create indirect emoluments;

(5) Strategic assets of the United States — including military positioning decisions, tariff structures, regulatory enforcement priorities, and foreign aid allocations — have documented monetary value to private parties, and decisions affecting those assets by officials with undisclosed financial interests in the outcomes constitute a form of public corruption regardless of whether a quid pro quo is proven;

(6) The revolving door between senior regulatory positions and the industries those positions regulate undermines the structural independence of federal enforcement and creates a rational expectation among Covered Entities that regulatory capture is achievable through employment offers, thereby suppressing enforcement prior to departure;

(7) A comprehensive statutory framework addressing beneficial ownership transparency, foreign business prohibition during tenure, emoluments enforcement with private right of action, and post-service cooling-off periods is necessary to protect the integrity of governmental decision-making and restore public trust in federal governance;

(8) In 2025 and 2026, the Government Accountability Office opened more than forty-five investigations into the withholding of appropriated funds and issued multiple decisions finding that executive agencies had violated the Impoundment Control Act of 1974. In August 2025, the executive branch transmitted a rescission proposal so close to the end of the fiscal year that the funds would expire before Congress could act, the first such "pocket rescission" since 1977, and a Federal court of appeals held that the Impoundment Control Act cannot be enforced by private parties;

(9) In January 2025, at least seventeen Inspectors General were removed without the thirty-day notice and case-specific reasons required by law. In September 2025, a Federal district court found the removals unlawful but concluded that it could not order reinstatement, leaving the statutory protection without an effective remedy;

(10) Senior officials and members of their families have issued, promoted, and profited from digital assets whose value is affected by the policies those officials set, and existing conflict-of-interest and disclosure laws do not reach those arrangements; and

(11) These failures are structural, not partisan. Each can recur under any administration, and each requires a statutory remedy that does not depend on the goodwill of the officials it constrains.

(b) PURPOSE.—The purpose of this Title is to establish enforceable statutory standards for the financial conduct of senior federal officials and their immediate family members during and following federal service, to create a civil action to enforce the Emoluments Clauses, to restrict post-service lobbying and digital asset conflicts, to secure Congress's power of the purse against unlawful impoundment, to protect the independence of Inspectors General, to strengthen payment integrity, and to eliminate structural conflicts of interest that compromise governmental policy-making.

Sec. 3502. Definitions — Title XXXV

As used in this Title:

(1) COVERED OFFICIAL.—The President, Vice President, any Cabinet Secretary, Deputy Secretary, Under Secretary, or Assistant Secretary of any executive department, any member of the White House senior staff, any member of the National Security Council, any independent agency head, and any other executive branch official confirmed by the Senate or serving in a position with a salary at or above the Executive Schedule Level IV rate.

(2) IMMEDIATE FAMILY MEMBER.—The spouse, domestic partner, child, sibling, or parent of a Covered Official, and any entity in which such a person holds a controlling interest or serves in a management capacity.

(3) FOREIGN GOVERNMENT-LINKED ENTERPRISE.—Any enterprise in which a foreign government, foreign sovereign wealth fund, foreign state-owned enterprise, or any entity in which a foreign government holds a direct or indirect ownership interest of ten percent (10%) or more, holds an ownership, licensing, revenue-sharing, or advisory interest.

(4) OFFICIAL INTEREST ENTERPRISE.—Any domestic entity, including a corporation, limited liability company, partnership, trust, or nonprofit organization, in which a Covered Official or Immediate Family Member holds a financial interest of five percent (5%) or more, serves in a management or advisory capacity, or from which a Covered Official or Immediate Family Member has received compensation exceeding ten thousand dollars ($10,000) in any twelve-month period within the four (4) years preceding the Covered Official's appointment.

Sec. 3503. Financial Conflict of Interest Prohibitions

(a) DIVESTITURE REQUIREMENT.—Every Covered Official shall, within ninety (90) days of assuming office, divest all financial interests in any Official Interest Enterprise that presents a direct conflict with the Covered Official's official duties, as determined by the Office of Government Ethics. Divestiture shall be into a qualified blind trust meeting the standards of section 13104(f) of title 5, United States Code, or through outright sale at fair market value.

(b) FOREIGN ENTERPRISE PROHIBITION.—No Covered Official may hold, directly or through an Immediate Family Member, any financial interest in a Foreign Government-Linked Enterprise during the Covered Official's term of service. Any such interest held at the time of appointment shall be divested within sixty (60) days of enactment or appointment, whichever is later.

(c) ENFORCEMENT.—The Office of Government Ethics shall publish a quarterly Public Integrity Compliance Report listing all Covered Officials, their divestiture status, and any outstanding compliance deficiencies. The FMIA shall cross-reference financial disclosure filings with its enterprise database to identify undisclosed interests and transmit findings to the Department of Justice Public Integrity Section within thirty (30) days of identification.

(d) PENALTIES.—Willful violation of this section by a Covered Official shall constitute a federal offense punishable by not more than five (5) years imprisonment, a civil fine of not less than one hundred thousand dollars ($100,000) per violation, and permanent disqualification from federal service.

Sec. 3504. Emoluments Clause Enforcement — Civil Action

(a) DEFINITION.—In this section, the term "emolument" means any payment, profit, gain, or other advantage, whether direct or through an Immediate Family Member or a Official Interest Enterprise, received by a Covered Official from—

(1) a foreign government, foreign sovereign wealth fund, foreign state-owned enterprise, or Foreign Government-Linked Enterprise, without the consent of Congress, within the meaning of Article I, Section 9, Clause 8 of the Constitution; or

(2) in the case of the President, the United States, any State, or any agency or instrumentality of either, other than the compensation fixed by law, within the meaning of Article II, Section 1, Clause 7 of the Constitution.

(b) CONSENT OF CONGRESS.—Consent of Congress under Article I, Section 9, Clause 8 may be given only by joint resolution that identifies the Covered Official, the source, and the value of the emolument.

(c) CIVIL ACTION.—A civil action for declaratory relief, injunctive relief, and disgorgement for a violation of subsection (a) may be brought in the United States District Court for the District of Columbia by—

(1) the Attorney General;

(2) any person who suffers a concrete competitive or economic injury from the violation;

(3) any State whose proprietary interests are injured by the violation; or

(4) either House of Congress, through its counsel, when authorized by resolution of that House.

(d) REMEDIES.—A court finding a violation shall order disgorgement to the Treasury of the value received, and may enjoin the receipt of further emoluments from the same source. The court may award reasonable attorney's fees to a prevailing plaintiff other than the United States.

(e) EXPEDITED REVIEW.—The court shall expedite any action under this section to the greatest possible extent. An interlocutory or final order shall be reviewable by appeal to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, which shall likewise expedite the appeal.

(f) DISCLOSURE.—Each Covered Official shall disclose, within thirty (30) days of receipt, any payment of more than one thousand dollars ($1,000) received from a source described in subsection (a)(1), whether directly or through an Immediate Family Member or a Official Interest Enterprise. The Office of Government Ethics shall publish each disclosure within five (5) business days.

Sec. 3505. Post-Service Restrictions

(a) COOLING-OFF PERIOD.—For two (2) years after leaving office, a former Covered Official shall not—

(1) knowingly make, with the intent to influence, any communication to or appearance before any officer or employee of the department or agency in which the former Covered Official served, on behalf of any person other than the United States; or

(2) accept compensation from any person over whose matters the former Covered Official participated personally and substantially in an official enforcement, contracting, licensing, or grant decision during the final two (2) years of service.

(b) FOREIGN PRINCIPALS.—A former Covered Official shall not at any time act as an agent of a foreign government or foreign political party, within the meaning of the Foreign Agents Registration Act of 1938 (22 U.S.C. 611 et seq.).

(c) RELATIONSHIP TO EXISTING LAW.—This section supplements, and does not limit, section 207 of title 18, United States Code. Where that section imposes a longer or broader restriction, it controls.

(d) PENALTY.—A violation of this section is punishable as provided in section 216 of title 18, United States Code.

Sec. 3506. Digital Asset Conflicts of Interest

(a) DEFINITIONS.—In this section:

(1) DIGITAL ASSET.—The term "digital asset" means any digital representation of value recorded on a cryptographically secured distributed ledger, including a payment stablecoin, a token, and a non-fungible token.

(2) COVERED PERSON.—The term "covered person" means a Covered Official, a Member of Congress, or an Immediate Family Member of either.

(b) PROHIBITIONS.—During the term of service of the relevant Covered Official or Member of Congress, no covered person may—

(1) issue, sponsor, or co-sponsor a digital asset;

(2) endorse or promote a digital asset or a digital asset business for compensation, or license a name, likeness, or brand to any such asset or business; or

(3) hold an ownership, revenue-sharing, or management interest in a person that issues a digital asset or operates a digital asset exchange, custodian, or payment stablecoin business.

(c) DIVESTITURE.—An interest prohibited by subsection (b)(3) held on the date of enactment, or on assuming office, shall be divested within ninety (90) days in the manner provided in Section 3503(a).

(d) DISCLOSURE.—Each Covered Official and Member of Congress shall report every acquisition or disposition of a digital asset by a covered person, with a value of more than one thousand dollars ($1,000), within thirty (30) days, in the manner required for securities transactions under chapter 131 of title 5, United States Code.

(e) PENALTIES.—Willful violation of subsection (b) is punishable as provided in Section 3503(d). Any profit derived from a violation shall be disgorged to the Treasury.

Sec. 3507. Appropriations Integrity and Impoundment Enforcement

(a) NO POCKET RESCISSIONS.—Notwithstanding section 1012 of the Impoundment Control Act of 1974 (2 U.S.C. 683), budget authority proposed for rescission in a special message transmitted fewer than forty-five (45) days of continuous session before that budget authority would expire may not be withheld from obligation pending congressional action, and shall be made available for obligation in time to be obligated before it expires.

(b) AVAILABILITY PRESERVED.—Budget authority that is withheld in violation of the Impoundment Control Act of 1974, as determined by a court or by the Comptroller General, shall remain available for obligation for a period after release equal to the period during which it was withheld, notwithstanding the expiration of its original period of availability.

(c) ENFORCEMENT BY THE COMPTROLLER GENERAL.—In any civil action brought by the Comptroller General under section 1016 of the Impoundment Control Act of 1974 (2 U.S.C. 687), the court shall expedite the action, and the action may be filed immediately upon transmittal of the Comptroller General's explanatory statement to Congress.

(d) ENFORCEMENT BY AFFECTED PARTIES.—A State, unit of local government, Indian tribe, or other intended recipient of budget authority withheld in violation of the Impoundment Control Act of 1974 may bring a civil action in a district court of the United States to compel its obligation in accordance with law.

(e) INFORMATION ACCESS.—The head of each executive agency shall provide the Comptroller General with any information, documents, and access to personnel requested in connection with a review under the Impoundment Control Act of 1974 within twenty (20) days of the request.

(f) PUBLIC APPORTIONMENTS.—The Director of the Office of Management and Budget shall post each apportionment and reapportionment of appropriations under section 1513 of title 31, United States Code, including all footnotes, on a public website not later than two (2) business days after approval, and shall maintain that website continuously.

Sec. 3508. Inspector General Independence

(a) REMOVAL WITHOUT REQUIRED NOTICE.—A removal or transfer of an Inspector General that does not comply with the notice and reasons requirements of section 403(b) of title 5, United States Code, shall not take effect until thirty (30) days after the date on which those requirements are met. Until then, the Inspector General shall remain in office with full pay and authority.

(b) BACK PAY.—An Inspector General removed in violation of subsection (a) shall be entitled to pay and benefits for the period of noncompliance.

(c) BUDGET AND ACCESS.—No officer or employee of an agency may restrict an Inspector General's access to records, personnel, or appropriated funds as a means of impeding an audit or investigation. The Council of the Inspectors General on Integrity and Efficiency shall report to Congress within seven (7) days any such restriction.

(d) ANNUAL REPORT.—The Council of the Inspectors General on Integrity and Efficiency shall report annually to Congress on all removals, transfers, placements on leave, and vacancies of Inspectors General, and on the duration of each vacancy.

(e) SEVERABILITY.—If subsection (a) is held invalid as applied to any removal, the notice and reasons requirements of section 403(b) of title 5, United States Code, shall continue to apply, and subsections (b) through (d) shall remain in effect.

Sec. 3509. Payment Integrity

(a) MANDATORY ESTIMATES.—Each Federal program that makes payments of more than one hundred million dollars ($100,000,000) in any fiscal year shall be treated as susceptible to significant improper payments for purposes of subchapter IV of chapter 33 of title 31, United States Code, and shall report an annual improper payment estimate, notwithstanding any other provision of law.

(b) DATA ANALYTICS CENTER.—The FMIA shall operate a permanent payment integrity analytics center, available to every executive agency and Inspector General, to identify improper payments and fraud before disbursement.

(c) RECOVERIES.—Improper payments recovered as a result of the analytics center shall be deposited in the SIRF in accordance with Section 201(b)(2), except where another law requires their return to the program or trust fund from which they were paid.

Sec. 3510. Public Accountability — Schedule D Benchmarks

The FMIA shall report biennially to Congress on: (1) number of Covered Officials achieving full divestiture compliance within the required window; (2) number and disposition of identified undisclosed financial interests referred to the Department of Justice Public Integrity Section; (3) number of civil actions filed under Section 3504 and their outcomes, and emolument disclosures published; (4) number of Foreign Government-Linked Enterprise interests divested under Section 3503(b); (5) compliance with the post-service restrictions of Section 3505 among departing Covered Officials; (6) digital asset divestitures and disclosures under Section 3506; (7) Comptroller General impoundment decisions, actions filed, and budget authority restored under Section 3507; (8) Inspector General removals, vacancies, and access restrictions reported under Section 3508; and (9) improper payment estimates, programs newly reporting, and recoveries under Section 3509.

Title XXXVI · Foreign Influence Transparency and Adversary Capital Defense

Sec. 3601. Findings and Purpose

(a) FINDINGS.—Congress finds that:

(1) The integrity of governmental policy-making depends on transparency as to which foreign principals are directing, funding, or participating in efforts to influence United States legislation, regulation, procurement, and public opinion, and existing law contains structural gaps that permit such influence to remain undisclosed;

(2) The Lobbying Disclosure Act of 1995 (2 U.S.C. § 1601 et seq.) exemption to the Foreign Agents Registration Act (22 U.S.C. § 611 et seq.) ("FARA") permits a person lobbying in the United States on behalf of a foreign principal to register under the less rigorous Lobbying Disclosure Act in lieu of FARA, thereby avoiding FARA's more comprehensive disclosure of the foreign principal relationship, the activities undertaken, and the compensation received;

(3) This Title regulates conduct — the undisclosed direction, funding, or control of influence activity by foreign principals, and the acquisition of sensitive United States assets by capital traceable to foreign adversaries — and does not regulate, restrict, or disfavor any person on the basis of national origin, ethnicity, religion, or the viewpoint or content of protected speech;

(4) The disclosure obligations of this Title apply with equal force to influence activity directed, funded, or controlled by any foreign government, whether an ally, a partner, a neutral, or a designated adversary of the United States, and impose no obligation by reason of the identity of any domestic ethnic, national-origin, or religious community;

(5) The Committee on Foreign Investment in the United States lacks comprehensive, categorical authority over the full range of acquisitions, greenfield investments, and controlling interests by which capital traceable to foreign adversaries may acquire United States critical infrastructure, defense-relevant supply chains, strategic compute capacity, agricultural land, and real property proximate to sensitive national security facilities; and

(6) Sanctions-evasion and asymmetric capital-flight networks routinely employ shell entities, front companies, and nominee ownership structures established in high-risk jurisdictions to obscure the beneficial ownership of capital and to defeat enforcement, and a beneficial-ownership look-through standard is necessary to give effect to the capital-flight lock authority of Section 1808.

(b) PURPOSE.—The purpose of this Title is to require uniform, viewpoint-neutral and nationality-neutral disclosure of foreign-principal influence activity, to close the Lobbying Disclosure Act exemption with respect to foreign-government principals, to establish a categorical screen on the acquisition of sensitive United States assets by capital traceable to foreign adversaries, and to extend a beneficial-ownership look-through standard to the enforcement authorities of this Act.

(c) RULE OF CONSTRUCTION — FIRST AMENDMENT AND EQUAL PROTECTION.—Nothing in this Title shall be construed to prohibit, penalize, or burden protected speech, association, petition, or the expression of any viewpoint, or to impose any obligation on any person by reason of that person's race, ethnicity, national origin, religion, or membership in any community. This Title imposes disclosure and screening obligations triggered solely by conduct, namely a documented relationship of foreign-principal direction, funding, or control, or a documented chain of beneficial ownership traceable to a foreign adversary. Each obligation under this Title shall be construed and applied as the least restrictive means of achieving the compelling governmental interest in transparency of foreign influence and protection of public assets, consistent with the First Amendment and the equal protection component of the Fifth Amendment.

Sec. 3602. Definitions — Title XXXVI

As used in this Title:

(1) FOREIGN PRINCIPAL.—The term has the meaning given in 22 U.S.C. § 611(b), and includes any foreign government, foreign political party, foreign-government-controlled entity, and any person outside the United States acting at the direction or under the control of any of the foregoing.

(2) FOREIGN ADVERSARY.—Any foreign government or regime designated as a foreign adversary by the Secretary of Commerce under 15 C.F.R. § 7.4, or otherwise designated as a foreign adversary by Federal statute or by Executive determination published in the Federal Register. The designation shall be made by reference to documented governmental conduct and not by reference to the population, diaspora, or ethnicity of any nation. As of the date of enactment, this term includes the governments identified in the prevailing Federal foreign-adversary designation; the operative list is the published designation in effect at the time of any enforcement action under this Title.

(3) ADVERSARY-TRACEABLE CAPITAL.—Capital in which a Foreign Adversary, an instrumentality of a Foreign Adversary, or a person owned or controlled by a Foreign Adversary holds, directly or through any chain of intermediary entities, a beneficial ownership interest of twenty-five percent (25%) or more, or over which such a person exercises control irrespective of the nominal percentage of ownership.

(4) SENSITIVE UNITED STATES ASSET.—(A) critical infrastructure as defined in 42 U.S.C. § 5195c(e); (B) any element of a defense-relevant supply chain certified as such by the Secretary of Defense; (C) strategic compute capacity within the meaning of Title XXXIII; (D) agricultural land exceeding three hundred twenty (320) contiguous acres; and (E) real property located within twenty-five (25) miles of a military installation, defense research facility, or other facility designated as sensitive by the Secretary of Defense.

(5) BENEFICIAL OWNER.—The term has the meaning given in 31 U.S.C. § 5336(a)(3), determined on a look-through basis through every intermediary entity, nominee, trust, and front company, without regard to the jurisdiction of organization of any intermediary.

(6) COVERED INFLUENCE ACTIVITY.—Any lobbying activity within the meaning of 2 U.S.C. § 1602, and any public-relations, political-consulting, or research-and-advocacy activity intended to influence United States Government policy or public opinion on a matter of public concern, that is directed, funded, supervised, or controlled, in whole or in part, by a Foreign Principal.

Sec. 3603. Closure of the Lobbying Disclosure Act Exemption for Foreign-Government Principals

(a) ELIMINATION OF EXEMPTION.—Section 3(h) of the Foreign Agents Registration Act (22 U.S.C. § 613(h)) is amended so that the exemption for persons registered under the Lobbying Disclosure Act shall not apply to any person engaged in Covered Influence Activity on behalf of, or under the direction or control of, a foreign government or foreign political party. Such a person shall register and report under FARA.

(b) PARITY OF DISCLOSURE — ALL FOREIGN GOVERNMENTS.—The obligation established in subsection (a) applies identically to influence activity directed, funded, or controlled by any foreign government, without distinction between allied, partner, neutral, or adversary governments. No foreign government's directed influence activity shall be exempt from FARA registration by reason of an alliance, treaty, defense, intelligence, or trade relationship with the United States.

(c) PARTICIPATION DISCLOSURE.—Any registrant under the Lobbying Disclosure Act shall disclose, in each periodic report, the identity of any foreign government entity or foreign political party that participates in the direction, planning, supervision, or control of the registrant's lobbying activities, regardless of whether that entity finances the activities. This subsection codifies the disclosure standard and applies uniformly to all foreign governments.

(d) NO REGULATION OF DOMESTIC ADVOCACY.—Nothing in this section requires registration or disclosure by any United States person engaged in advocacy that is not directed, funded, supervised, or controlled by a Foreign Principal. The sharing of a policy viewpoint with, or alignment of a policy position with that of, a foreign government does not, without a documented relationship of direction, funding, supervision, or control, constitute Covered Influence Activity.

Sec. 3604. Foreign-Funded Influence Disclosure — Research, Advocacy, and Media

(a) DISCLOSURE REQUIREMENT.—Any organization exempt from taxation under section 501(c) of the Internal Revenue Code, and any media or research organization, that produces or disseminates research, analysis, or advocacy intended to influence United States Government policy and that has received aggregate funding of fifty thousand dollars ($50,000) or more from one or more Foreign Principals within the preceding twelve (12) months, shall disclose the identity of each such Foreign Principal and the aggregate amount received, in a public filing with the Department of Justice and in a conspicuous notation on the published product.

(b) NATIONALITY NEUTRALITY.—The disclosure obligation of this section applies to funding from any Foreign Principal of any nation, and creates no obligation triggered by the national origin, ethnicity, or religion of the organization's members, donors, or staff.

(c) NO CONTENT RESTRICTION.—This section imposes no restriction on the content, viewpoint, or conclusions of any research, analysis, or advocacy. It requires only disclosure of the funding relationship.

Sec. 3605. Adversary Capital Screen on Sensitive United States Assets

(a) SCREEN ESTABLISHED.—No person shall acquire a controlling interest in, or a greenfield ownership position in, any Sensitive United States Asset using Adversary-Traceable Capital, except upon prior review and written clearance by the Committee on Foreign Investment in the United States or a successor interagency body designated by the President.

(b) MANDATORY NOTIFICATION.—Any acquisition described in subsection (a) shall be notified to the Committee not less than forty-five (45) days before consummation, with full disclosure of the beneficial ownership chain determined on the look-through basis of Section 3602(5).

(c) CATEGORICAL RESTRICTION.—Where the Committee determines that a proposed acquisition would place a Sensitive United States Asset under the control of Adversary-Traceable Capital and that no mitigation measure can adequately protect the national security interest, the Committee shall prohibit the acquisition. Such a determination shall be supported by written findings and is subject to judicial review under the procedural protections of Title XVIII.

(d) DIVESTITURE OF EXISTING HOLDINGS.—Where Adversary-Traceable Capital holds, as of the date of enactment, a controlling interest in a Sensitive United States Asset, the holder shall notify the Committee within one hundred eighty (180) days, and the Committee may order divestiture subject to the one hundred eighty (180) day asset-divestment notice window and ALJ Judicial Verification Warrant requirements of Section 1806, and, where applicable, the volume-absorption and just-compensation protections of Title XIV.

(e) ALLY AND PARTNER CAPITAL UNAFFECTED.—Capital traceable solely to a foreign government that is not a Foreign Adversary is not Adversary-Traceable Capital and is not subject to the screen of this section, except to the extent independently reviewable under existing Committee authority.

Sec. 3606. Beneficial-Ownership Look-Through — Anti-Front-Company Enforcement

(a) LOOK-THROUGH STANDARD.—For purposes of the capital-flight lock authority of Section 1808, the asset-seizure and forced-divestment authorities of Title XVIII, and the adversary capital screen of Section 3605, beneficial ownership shall be determined on a look-through basis that pierces every intermediary entity, nominee arrangement, trust, and front company, without regard to the jurisdiction in which any intermediary is organized.

(b) FRONT-COMPANY PRESUMPTION.—An entity shall be presumed to be a front company for a documented beneficial owner where the entity (1) is organized in a jurisdiction identified as high-risk for money laundering by the Financial Action Task Force or the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network; (2) lacks substantial independent business operations; and (3) engages in transactions principally for the benefit of the documented beneficial owner. The presumption is rebuttable by clear and convincing evidence of independent operations.

(c) NO STRICT-LIABILITY EXPANSION.—This section establishes a method of determining beneficial ownership and does not create any new penalty. All penal remedies arising from conduct identified through the look-through standard remain subject to the Article III adjudication and jury-trial requirements applicable under this Act, and to the pre-deprivation notice and warrant requirements of Title XVIII.

Sec. 3607. Enforcement and Coordination

(a) PRIMARY AGENCIES.—The Department of Justice (FARA Unit) shall be the primary enforcement agency for Sections 3603 and 3604; the Committee on Foreign Investment in the United States shall be primary for Section 3605; and the Department of the Treasury, in coordination with the FMIA, shall be primary for Section 3606 as it relates to Section 1808.

(b) CIVIL DISCLOSURE ENFORCEMENT.—A knowing and willful failure to make a disclosure required by Section 3603 or 3604 is subject to civil penalty. Consistent with the enforcement architecture of this Act, no civil monetary penalty shall be finally assessed except by a United States District Court with the Seventh Amendment right to trial by jury preserved.

(c) NO RETALIATORY OR SELECTIVE ENFORCEMENT.—Enforcement under this Title shall be conducted on a viewpoint-neutral and nationality-neutral basis. The Department of Justice shall publish an annual report stating, in the aggregate and by foreign-government principal, the number of registrations, disclosures, and enforcement actions under this Title, to permit public verification that enforcement does not target any nation, viewpoint, or community selectively.

Sec. 3608. Schedule D Benchmarks — Title XXXVI

The Department of Justice and the FMIA shall report biennially to Congress on: (1) the number of foreign-government principals newly registered under FARA following closure of the Lobbying Disclosure Act exemption; (2) the number of foreign-funded influence disclosures filed under Section 3604; (3) the number of adversary-capital acquisitions notified, cleared, mitigated, or prohibited under Section 3605; (4) the number of beneficial-ownership look-through determinations made under Section 3606; and (5) confirmation, with supporting aggregate data, that enforcement has been conducted on a nationality-neutral and viewpoint-neutral basis.

Title XXXIX · Digital Government Modernization

Sec. 3901. Findings and Purpose

(a) FINDINGS.—Congress finds that:

(1) The United States ranked first in the United Nations E-Government Development Index in 2003 through 2005 and nineteenth in 2024.

(2) Core benefit, tax, and payment systems at major federal agencies, and many State unemployment insurance systems, still run on decades-old code that is costly to maintain, difficult to secure, and prone to failure during surges in demand.

(3) The Government Accountability Office estimated federal improper payments at approximately one hundred eighty-six billion dollars ($186,000,000,000) in fiscal year 2025, and has repeatedly linked improper payments to outdated systems and the inability to verify eligibility data across agencies.

(4) Americans should be able to apply for and manage federal benefits online in one place, securely and without repeating the same information to multiple agencies.

(b) PURPOSE.—The purpose of this Title is to retire high-risk legacy systems on a fixed schedule, give the public a single secure front door to federal services, and reduce fraud and improper payments through modern data verification, while protecting privacy.

Sec. 3902. Legacy System Retirement

(a) INVENTORY.—Not later than one hundred eighty (180) days after the date of enactment of this Act, the head of each agency listed in section 901(b) of title 31, United States Code, shall submit to the Federal Chief Information Officer and to Congress an inventory of every mission-critical information system more than twenty (20) years old or dependent on programming languages or hardware no longer supported by a vendor.

(b) RETIREMENT SCHEDULE.—Each agency shall retire or modernize every system in its inventory within seven (7) years, and shall give priority to systems that process Social Security, Medicare, Medicaid, veterans', tax, and unemployment insurance payments.

(c) LEGACY MODERNIZATION FUND.—There is established within the Technology Modernization Fund under section 1078 of the Modernizing Government Technology Act of 2017 (40 U.S.C. § 11301 note) a Legacy Retirement Account. Awards from the Account shall be repaid by the receiving agency from documented operating savings over not more than ten (10) years.

(d) STATE UNEMPLOYMENT SYSTEMS.—The Secretary of Labor shall award grants to States to replace legacy unemployment insurance systems with modular, open-standard systems capable of handling claim surges of not less than ten (10) times normal volume, with fraud-detection capabilities meeting standards set by the Secretary.

(e) MODERN PRACTICES.—Modernization funded under this section shall use modular contracting, agile and user-centered development, open standards that avoid vendor lock-in, and the cybersecurity and post-quantum migration requirements of Title XVI.

Sec. 3903. Single Front Door for Federal Services

(a) ESTABLISHMENT.—The Administrator of General Services, through the Technology Transformation Services, shall establish a single, secure online portal through which an individual may apply for, track, and manage federal benefits and services, using the Login.gov identity-verification service administered by the General Services Administration or its successor.

(b) TELL US ONCE.—With the individual's affirmative consent, information verified by one agency may be reused by another agency for the same individual, so that no individual is required to resubmit information the Federal Government already holds.

(c) ACCESSIBILITY.—The portal shall be usable on mobile devices, shall meet the accessibility requirements of section 508 of the Rehabilitation Act of 1973, and shall offer in-person and telephone alternatives for individuals who cannot or choose not to use online services.

(d) PRIVACY.—Data shared under this section shall be limited to the minimum necessary, shall be subject to the Privacy Act of 1974 and the data-rights and warrant protections of Title III, shall not be used for any law-enforcement purpose except pursuant to a warrant, and shall not be sold or transferred to any non-governmental person.

Sec. 3904. Payment Verification and Fraud Prevention

(a) VERIFICATION SERVICE.—The Secretary of the Treasury shall expand the Do Not Pay working system under section 3354 of title 31, United States Code, to provide real-time eligibility and identity verification to federal agencies and to States administering federally funded programs, including access to the Social Security Administration's full death records.

(b) COORDINATION WITH PAYMENT INTEGRITY.—Savings documented under this section shall be reported under the payment integrity requirements of Section 3509.

Sec. 3905. Funding and Budget Neutrality

(a) SOURCE.—There are authorized to be appropriated for the Legacy Retirement Account and grants under section 3902 five billion dollars ($5,000,000,000) for each of fiscal years 2028 through 2032, derived as follows: first, from improper-payment reductions documented under Section 3509 and section 3904; and second, to the extent such savings are insufficient, from the Tier 3 capacity of the SIRF identified in Schedule G, section 4, certified by the Director of the Office of Management and Budget as not increasing the deficit, using the Scenario B conservative floor.

(b) REPAYMENT.—Repayments to the Legacy Retirement Account shall remain available for further modernization awards.

Sec. 3906. Schedule D Benchmarks — Title XXXIX

The Federal Chief Information Officer shall report biennially to Congress on: (1) the number of inventoried legacy systems retired or modernized, by agency, against the schedule of section 3902; (2) operating savings and repayments to the Legacy Retirement Account; (3) the number of services and individuals using the portal under section 3903, completion times, and user satisfaction; (4) improper payments prevented through section 3904; (5) the United States ranking in the United Nations E-Government Development Index; (6) privacy complaints and their resolution; and (7) under section 3907, the number of single applications completed, States participating, duplicate enrollments ended, and costs and savings recognized under Section 3412.

Sec. 3907. One Benefits Application and Shared Services

(a) PURPOSE.—To let an individual apply once for the federal benefits the individual may be eligible for, to stop duplicate and mistaken payments by sharing verification across programs, and to lower the cost of running those programs, without merging any program, its trust fund, or its eligibility rules.

(b) ONE APPLICATION.—Not later than three (3) years after the date of enactment, the portal established under section 3903 shall offer a single application that screens an individual or household for Social Security benefits and Supplemental Security Income, Medicare, Medicaid and the Children's Health Insurance Program, the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program, the Basic Minimum Plan and coverage through an Exchange, and other federal benefits designated by the Director of the Office of Management and Budget. Screening is not a determination: each program shall determine eligibility and benefits under its own law, and eligibility for one program shall not by itself establish eligibility for another.

(c) STATE CHOICE.—A State administering Medicaid, the Children's Health Insurance Program, or the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program may accept application information and verification results provided under this section in place of its own. No State is required to participate. The Federal Government shall bear the cost of building and operating the shared application and verification services, and nothing in this section shifts any administrative or benefit cost to a State.

(d) SHARED VERIFICATION.—The verification service under section 3904 shall answer queries from federal agencies and participating States on identity, death, income, and enrollment in other federally funded programs, including enrollment in Medicaid in more than one State and simultaneous enrollment in Medicaid and subsidized Exchange or Basic Minimum Plan coverage. The service shall—

(1) answer specific queries and shall not create or maintain a single combined file of individuals' records;

(2) log every query, and make the log available to the Inspectors General of the agencies concerned and to the individual whose record was queried, on request;

(3) comply with the Privacy Act of 1974, the computer matching requirements of section 552a(o) through (u) of title 5, United States Code, and section 3903(d) of this Act; and

(4) be audited each year by the Comptroller General for accuracy, security, and misuse.

(e) DUE PROCESS.—No benefit shall be denied, reduced, suspended, or terminated on the basis of a match under this section alone. The individual shall receive written notice of the information relied on and not less than thirty (30) days to respond, and a person shall review the case before any adverse action, consistent with Title XVIII.

(f) DUPLICATE COVERAGE.—Not less than once each quarter, the Administrator of the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services and participating States shall reconcile enrollment to identify individuals enrolled in more than one State's Medicaid program or in both Medicaid and subsidized coverage, and shall end duplicate federal payments after notice under subsection (e).

(g) SHARED SERVICES.—The Director of the Office of Management and Budget shall designate shared-service providers for contact centers, identity proofing, document intake, and payment processing for the programs listed in subsection (b). An agency shall use a designated provider when the Comptroller General finds that doing so lowers cost without lowering service quality.

(h) PROTECTIONS.—Nothing in this section—

(1) alters the status of the Social Security Administration as an independent agency or the separate accounting of any trust fund;

(2) merges, eliminates, or changes the eligibility rules or benefits of any program; or

(3) authorizes the use of program data for any law-enforcement purpose except pursuant to a warrant.

(i) FUNDING AND SAVINGS.—Costs under this section shall be met within the amounts authorized by section 3905. Savings under this section are recognized only as provided in Section 3412 and shall be reported under section 3906.

Schedules

Schedule a — the Federal Data Veracity Matrix Algorithmic Specification
(Enacted as a statutory annex to preserve Non-Delegation durability under Gundy v. United States, 139 S. Ct. 2116 (2019) and West Virginia v. EPA, 597 U.S. 697 (2022))


1. MATHEMATICAL VARIANCE CORE.—The automated tracking of structural variance for any monitored socioeconomic or physical asset layer shall operate via continuous telemetry mapping against a rolling historical baseline (Br).

2. THE TRIGGER EQUATION.—A reportable structural variance is hardcoded as:

   |Tm - Br|
   --------- x 100 ≥ 15.0%
      Br

Where Tm represents real-time material telemetry ingested via un-tamperable digital data pipelines. If ΔS ≥ 15.0%, the Structured Accountability Engine is engaged with zero human administrative intervention authorized.

3. MULTI-LAYER VARIANCE COMPOSITE.—For sectors monitored across multiple simultaneous data layers (e.g., both pricing and supply volume for pharmaceutical markets), the composite structural variance score shall be calculated as:

   ΔScomposite = √[(ΔS1² + ΔS2² + ... ΔSn²) / n]

   Where each ΔSi represents the individual layer variance. The composite threshold for trigger engagement is fifteen percent (15.0%).

4. FALSE POSITIVE MITIGATION.—Before generating a Verified Structural Variance Report, the FDVM algorithm shall apply a seven-day data smoothing window to distinguish genuine structural variances from transient market noise. Transient variances lasting fewer than seven (7) consecutive days shall not generate a Verified Structural Variance Report.

5. ALGORITHM TRANSPARENCY.—The full source code of the FDVM algorithm shall be published on a publicly accessible federal repository within ninety (90) days of enactment and updated within thirty (30) days of any modification.
Schedule B — Particularized Injury Specifications for Article III Standing
(Enacted as a statutory annex to satisfy TransUnion LLC v. Ramirez, 594 U.S. 413 (2021) and Spokeo, Inc. v. Robins, 578 U.S. 330 (2016))


1. TITLE I (Data Integrity).—Concrete injury is defined as the explicit deprivation of a citizen's or agency's right to accurate data within a federal regulatory determination, resulting in a materially different outcome than would have occurred with accurate data.

2. TITLE II (SIRF Diversion).—Concrete injury is defined as the calculable economic loss and foregone employment wages suffered by domestic industrial workers due to an unlawful diversion of SIRF capital, calculated as the per-worker share of the unlawfully diverted sum applied to the SIRF Investment Impact Model.

3. TITLE V (Universal Prosperity).—Concrete injury is defined as the deprivation of a statutory entitlement in a specific, calculable dollar amount, calculated by applying the applicable benefit formula of Title V or the designated mechanisms of Section 3403 to the claimant's verified household profile.

4. TITLE XIII (Pharmaceutical Parity).—Concrete injury is defined as the exact dollar difference between the price paid by a patient for a covered therapeutic and the G7 median price of the identical therapeutic at the time of purchase.

5. TITLE XIV (Shelter Security).—Concrete injury is defined as the precise monthly dollar difference between actual rent paid and the formula-defined Structural Rent Baseline within an over-concentrated housing market.

6. TITLE XV (Knowledge Access).—Concrete injury is defined as the exact nominal dollar value of student loan interest accrued under a superseded predatory structure after the effective date of this Act.
Schedule C — SIRF Investment Impact Model
(Enacted as a statutory annex for purposes of Sections 1801(a)(2) and 1902)


1. PURPOSE.—The SIRF Investment Impact Model (SIIM) provides the standardized methodology for calculating the economic harm caused to domestic workers, manufacturers, and communities by any unlawful diversion of SIRF capital, for purposes of establishing concrete, particularized injury under Article III.

2. SIIM FORMULA.—The per-worker economic harm (H) from unlawful SIRF diversion (D) in a designated facility or community is calculated as:

   H = D / [W x (1 + M)]

   Where:
   W = the number of affected domestic workers at the designated facility or in the affected community, as recorded in FDVM employment telemetry;
   M = the FMIA-published regional employment multiplier for the affected sector (representing indirect and induced employment effects), set at a floor of 1.5 and a ceiling of 3.0.

3. COMMUNITY HARM CALCULATION.—For community-level harm calculations where no specific facility is the locus of diversion, H shall be calculated using the total working-age population of the affected zip code or census tract as W, with M set at the FMIA-published regional average multiplier.

4. MINIMUM COGNIZABLE HARM.—No enforcement action under Section 1801(a)(2) shall be cognizable unless the calculated per-worker harm H exceeds five hundred dollars ($500) per affected worker. This floor is established to satisfy the concreteness requirement of TransUnion LLC v. Ramirez.
Schedule D — Performance Benchmarks for Biennial National Resilience Impact Report
(Enacted as a statutory annex for purposes of Sections 004(d) and 1804(b))


1. TITLE I BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) FDVM operational coverage of not less than 80% of designated critical asset layers within 36 months;
   (b) Not fewer than 100 Verified Structural Variance Reports generated annually after full deployment;
   (c) Median enforcement action initiated within 45 days of Verified Structural Variance Report.

2. TITLE II BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) SIRF capitalization of not less than $10 billion within 5 years;
   (b) Not fewer than 500 Main Street Entity grants disbursed annually;
   (c) SIRF administrative cost ratio not to exceed 3% of total SIRF assets.

3. TITLE V BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) Public Endowment Distribution deployed to not fewer than 5 million households within 3 years;
   (b) Measured reduction in food insecurity rate in pilot MSAs of not less than 15% within 2 years;
   (c) Secure Child Prosperity Trust accounts established for not fewer than 90% of eligible births within 2 years.

4. TITLE VII BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) No single Covered Entity controlling more than 12% of any designated agricultural category within 5 years;
   (b) Not fewer than 1,000 worker cooperatives or municipal food security trusts capitalized within 7 years;
   (c) National Caloric Reserve achieving 90-day supply buffer within 4 years.

5. TITLE IX BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) Not fewer than 10 SMR co-location projects in active construction within 5 years;
   (b) Average residential energy expenditure reduced by not less than 10% in SMR grid zones within 8 years;
   (c) NRC SMR expedited licensing track operational within 18 months of enactment.

6. TITLE XIII BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) Not fewer than 95% of covered therapeutic agents priced at or below G7 parity within 2 years;
   (b) Domestic API manufacturing share of FDA Essential Medicines List reaching not less than 50% within 5 years;
   (c) Average annual per-patient pharmaceutical expenditure reduction of not less than $800 within 5 years.

7. TITLE XIV BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) Institutional single-family portfolio concentration below 50 units per entity per MSA within 3 years;
   (b) Not fewer than 50,000 first-time homebuyers receiving federal down payment assistance within 5 years;
   (c) Community land trust housing inventory increased by not fewer than 100,000 units within 7 years.

8. TITLE XV BENCHMARKS.—
   (a) Zero interest accrual on all federal student loans achieved within 6 months of enactment;
   (b) Not fewer than 500,000 income-based forgiveness discharges within 3 years;
   (c) Public university in-state tuition frozen for not less than 90% of eligible institutions within 1 year.

9. HOUSEHOLD BENEFIT REPORTING BENCHMARK.—The FMIA shall publish, within one year of enactment and annually thereafter, the measured distribution of household benefits and costs under this Act by income quintile, household type, and State, as required by Section 1901(b).


EFFECTIVE DATE; IMPLEMENTATION PRIORITY SEQUENCE; PILOT CERTIFICATION
Schedule E — Phase Gate Trigger Parameters
SEC. E-001. PHASE GATE CONDITIONS — STATUTORY THRESHOLDS.

(a) PHASE 1 — DEFAULT POSITION.—Upon enactment, the Household Structural Relief Node shall be in Phase 1 status and shall fund the designated mechanisms of Section 3403 in their statutory priority order, to the extent of verified Node inflows.

(b) PHASE 2 — GATE 1 CONDITIONS.—The Household Structural Relief Node shall advance to Phase 2 — full statutory funding of the first three designated mechanisms of Section 3403 — when all three of the following conditions are simultaneously certified by the CBO in a formal cost estimate published in the Federal Register:

(1) DEFICIT CONDITION.—The projected annual federal deficit for the current fiscal year is below nine hundred billion dollars ($900,000,000,000) based on CBO's most recent baseline projection;

(2) TREND CONDITION.—CBO's ten-year baseline projection shows a declining deficit trajectory, with the projected deficit in Year 10 of the baseline lower than the current fiscal year's projected deficit; and

(3) SIRF CONDITION.—The SIRF balance, as certified by the FMIA, exceeds two hundred billion dollars ($200,000,000,000).

(c) PHASE 3 — GATE 2 CONDITIONS.—The Household Structural Relief Node shall advance to Phase 3 — full statutory funding of every designated mechanism of Section 3403 — when both of the following conditions are simultaneously certified by the CBO:

(1) DEBT-TO-GDP CONDITION.—The federal debt held by the public as a percentage of GDP is below one hundred ten percent (110%); and

(2) DEFICIT CONDITION.—The projected annual federal deficit for the current fiscal year is below four hundred billion dollars ($400,000,000,000).

(d) AUTOMATIC TRIGGER.—Phase Gate conditions are self-executing. When CBO certifies that all conditions for a given Phase Gate are met in a single published cost estimate or budget outlook, the corresponding Node funding expansion shall activate within sixty (60) days without requiring additional legislative action.

(e) DOWNWARD ADJUSTMENT.—If, after a Phase Gate has been met, fiscal conditions deteriorate such that the deficit rises above the applicable ceiling for two (2) consecutive fiscal years, Node funding for the mechanisms of Section 3403 shall be adjusted downward in reverse priority order per Section 005(b)(1), not eliminated entirely. The Phase Gate shall be re-met before returning to full Phase disbursement.

(f) REVENUE SUPPRESSION COUNTERFACTUAL ADJUSTMENT.—(1) DETECTION MANDATE.—The FMIA shall maintain a Revenue Suppression Detection Module (RSDM) that identifies coordinated domestic revenue contraction by Covered Entities exceeding fifteen percent (15%) of the prior 36-month rolling baseline, correlated across two or more entities in the same sector, within twelve (12) months of enactment or any FMIA enforcement action. (2) CBO COUNTERFACTUAL DEFICIT.—Upon FMIA certification of a Revenue Suppression Event, the CBO shall compute and publish a Revenue-Adjusted Counterfactual Deficit (RACD) that adds back estimated revenue suppressed by the verified evasion activity. (3) ALTERNATIVE GATE CERTIFICATION.—The Phase Gate 2 Deficit Condition is satisfied if either: (A) the actual projected deficit falls below $900,000,000,000; OR (B) the RACD falls below $900,000,000,000 and the FMIA has certified a Revenue Suppression Event affecting the standard calculation. (4) REVENUE SUPPRESSION ASSESSMENT.—Any Covered Entity engaging in coordinated revenue suppression to prevent Gate 1 certification shall be subject to a Revenue Suppression Assessment equal to three times (3x) the estimated suppression value, deposited into the SIRF.

(g) NO CASH DISTRIBUTION.—Consistent with Section 3401, no Phase of this Schedule authorizes a universal or recurring cash distribution to households. Phase advancement increases funding only for the designated mechanisms of Section 3403.
Schedule F — Pharmaceutical Compulsory Licensing Royalty Schedule
SEC. F-001. COMPULSORY LICENSING FRAMEWORK.

(a) REPLACEMENT OF PATENT RECAPTURE.—The automatic patent recapture mechanism formerly in Section 1301(b) of this Act is hereby superseded by the compulsory licensing framework established in this Schedule. Patent recapture — the involuntary termination of a patent holder's exclusive rights — shall not serve as an enforcement remedy for pharmaceutical pricing violations under this Act. Constitutional property rights of patent holders are preserved through the royalty structure established herein.

(b) COMPULSORY LICENSE ISSUANCE.—Upon a pricing violation as defined in Section 1301 of this Act — where a pharmaceutical Covered Entity prices a drug derived from NIH, BARDA, or DoD-funded research above the G7 parity ceiling for more than thirty (30) consecutive calendar days — HHS shall issue a compulsory license authorizing qualified generic manufacturers to produce and sell the drug in the United States at a price at or below the G7 parity ceiling.

(c) ROYALTY OBLIGATIONS.—The original patent holder shall receive ongoing royalty payments from all compulsory licensees at the following rates:

(1) STANDARD RATE.—Eight percent (8%) of the net sales revenue of the generic manufacturer, paid quarterly, for all drugs not qualifying for the orphan drug or pandemic emergency rates below.

(2) ORPHAN DRUG RATE.—Twelve percent (12%) of net sales revenue for drugs with FDA orphan drug designation under 21 U.S.C. § 360bb, reflecting the limited market size and higher per-unit development cost allocation.

(3) PANDEMIC EMERGENCY RATE.—Four percent (4%) of net sales revenue for compulsory licenses issued during a declared public health emergency under 42 U.S.C. § 247d, reflecting the national emergency public interest in maximum access.

(d) PATENT RETAINED.—Issuance of a compulsory license under this Schedule does not extinguish, transfer, diminish, or impair the original patent holder's underlying patent. The original patent holder retains full patent rights, including the right to license to additional parties, pursue infringement actions against unlicensed manufacturers, and receive the royalties specified herein. Compulsory licensing under this Schedule shall not constitute a taking requiring compensation under the Fifth Amendment, as the property right is preserved and ongoing royalty income is provided.

(e) LICENSE TERM.—A compulsory license issued under this Schedule shall remain in effect until the original patent holder reduces pricing to at or below the G7 parity ceiling and maintains compliant pricing for not less than ninety (90) consecutive calendar days, at which point the compulsory license shall automatically terminate.

(f) JUST COMPENSATION ARBITRATION.—

(1) RIGHT TO CHALLENGE.—Any patent holder subject to a compulsory license under this Schedule who believes the applicable royalty rate constitutes less than just compensation within the meaning of the Fifth Amendment to the Constitution may petition the United States Court of Federal Claims for a just compensation determination within one hundred eighty (180) days of compulsory license issuance.

(2) ARBITRATION PANEL.—Upon petition, the Court of Federal Claims shall convene a three-member Independent Pharmaceutical Royalty Arbitration Panel consisting of: one economist with expertise in pharmaceutical market valuation appointed by the court; one representative of the patent holder; and one representative of HHS. The Panel shall issue a binding just compensation determination within one hundred twenty (120) days of convening.

(3) ROYALTY FLOOR.—The arbitration panel's determination shall serve as the operative royalty rate if and only if it exceeds the rates specified in subsections (c)(1)-(3). The statutory rates in subsections (c)(1)-(3) serve as floors, not ceilings, for just compensation purposes.

(4) NO STAY.—Filing a petition under this subsection shall not stay the issuance or operation of the compulsory license. Access to the drug at the G7 parity ceiling price shall continue uninterrupted during arbitration proceedings.

(5) COORDINATION WITH 28 U.S.C. § 1498.—Nothing in this subsection limits the rights of patent holders under 28 U.S.C. § 1498 to seek compensation for government use of patents. This subsection provides an additional, supplementary arbitration pathway.
Schedule G — Revenue Assumptions and Methodology
(Enacted as a statutory annex for purposes of Sections 3402, 3406, and 1903)


1. PURPOSE.

Schedule G provides the line-by-line build-up of the revenue, program cost, and deficit-reduction figures stated in Section 3406 (Methodology version 2026.4, November 2026). Each revenue stream is estimated against published Congressional Budget Office, Joint Committee on Taxation, Treasury, and agency baselines for comparable policies. Scenario A is the central estimate. Scenario B is a conservative floor that discounts each stream by confidence class: High-confidence streams by five percent (5%), Moderate by twenty percent (20%), and Preliminary by fifty percent (50%). All figures are annual, in billions of first-year dollars, at full operational ramp unless otherwise stated. Official scores by the Congressional Budget Office and the Joint Committee on Taxation control upon publication.

  TOTAL PROGRAM COSTS, OUTLAYS, AND INFRASTRUCTURE                                                    $ 446.1B

  Basic Minimum Plan basis: approximately 25,500,000 newly covered enrollees (85 percent take-up of approximately 30,000,000 uninsured) at approximately $7,000 per enrollee at 100 percent of Medicare rates; plus approximately $40B for the move of transitioned Medicaid and CHIP enrollees to Medicare-level payment and approximately $41.5B for the move of Exchange enrollees to the BMP; less approximately $40B in enrollee premium contributions above 250 percent of the poverty line (Section 2110(a)); less approximately $15B in reduced federal uncompensated-care spending and approximately $25B in Title XIII pharmaceutical savings. State maintenance-of-effort contributions under Section 2103(d)(1) replace the former State share for transitioned populations.



4. RECONCILIATION AND TEN-YEAR PROJECTION.

  Full-ramp annual deficit reduction (Scenario A): $635.4B revenue - $446.1B costs and outlays = $189.3B.
  Revenue ramps with enforcement capacity (86, 94, 98, then 100 percent realization in Years 1-4) and grows 4 percent a year; programs phase in behind the certification gates of Schedule E (55, 75, 90, then 100 percent) and grow 4.5 percent a year; reduced debt lowers interest costs at 3.5 percent.

  Ten-year combined deficit reduction: $4.4T before interest savings; $5.0T including interest savings (range $4.0T to $5.7T).
  Growth effects of reshoring, permitting and infrastructure are not included. By the Congressional Budget Office's published rule of thumb, growth 0.1 percentage point a year faster than projected reduces ten-year deficits by roughly $300 billion.

  OPEN-STANDARDS SCORING: Individual income and payroll tax provisions (Secs. 2408, 2420(a), 3410(e)) were scored with the open-source Tax-Calculator model (Policy Simulation Library, version 6.9, public CPS file, current-law baseline including Public Law 119-21) and adjusted for behavioral response; Social Security provisions against published estimates of the SSA Office of the Chief Actuary (2025 Trustees Report); spending options against CBO, Options for Reducing the Deficit: 2025 to 2034. Scripts and inputs are published with the datasets.

  RELATIONSHIP TO SECTION 3406(f): The deficit reduction of Section 3406 lowers the CBO baseline deficit by roughly one-tenth, and Section 3410 roughly doubles that by the tenth year. The deficit limits of Section 3406(f) are reached through that reduction together with any Fiscal Target Act enacted by Congress or, failing enactment, the automatic adjustments of Section 3406(f)(4).


5. METHODOLOGY TRANSPARENCY AND EXTERNAL REVIEW.

(a) All revenue projections in this Schedule are grounded in publicly available CBO, JCT, IRS, FDIC, BEA, EIA, GAO, OECD, and independent academic sources. Each stream is independently scoreable by the Congressional Budget Office without reliance on NSRA-specific assumptions.

(b) No revenue stream in this Schedule relies on behavioral projections that assume corporate behavior changes in a direction favorable to revenue collection. All projections use current-law behavioral baselines. Revenue reductions from behavioral change (e.g., corporations reducing leveraged buyout activity in response to the LBO cap) are incorporated into the dynamic scoring figures where applicable.

(c) The FMIA shall publish an annual Schedule G Variance Report no later than March 15 of each year comparing actual SIRF revenue collections for the prior fiscal year to each stream's Schedule G projection, with a plain-language explanation of any variance exceeding ten percent (10%) of the projected amount. This Report shall be submitted to the Congressional Budget Office, the Joint Committee on Taxation, and made publicly available at no cost.

(d) The Congressional Budget Office is directed to score each revenue stream in this Schedule independently and to publish its findings, including any divergence from the statutory floor estimates stated herein, so that Congress and the public may evaluate the revenue architecture of this Act against an independent baseline. Where the Congressional Budget Office's independent score of any stream falls below the statutory floor stated in this Schedule, the FMIA shall, within ninety (90) days, transmit to Congress a reconciliation memorandum identifying the source of the divergence and any corrective calibration, without any reduction in the household benefit obligations established elsewhere in this Act.
